## **Appendix 1 – ERG preferences** 

ERG preferences have been implemented: 

- HR for ticagrelor vs. aspirin for health events are the same as HR for rivaroxaban vs. aspirin 

- Null transitions are imputed 

   1. Null event probabilities after a first event are imputed with the associated probability of the event-free health state 

||**Risk of MI after a first event**|
|---|---|
||**Base case**<br>**Null transition imputed**|
|**Event-free**|0.00290<br>0.00290|
|**Acute MI**|0.00641<br>0.00641|
|**Post-acute MI**|0.01852<br>0.01852|
|**Acute IS**|0<br>0.00290|
|**Post-acute IS**|0.00356<br>0.00356|
|**Acute ICH**|0<br>0.00290|
|**Post-acute ICH**|0<br>0.00290|



2. Null death probabilities after a first event are imputed with the associated probability of the event-free health state 

||**Mortality risk of Stroke after a first event**|
|---|---|
||**Base case**<br>**Null transition imputed**|
|**Event-free**|0.00017<br>0.00017|
|**Acute MI**|0<br>0.00017|
|**Post-acute MI**|0<br>0.00017|
|**Acute IS**|0.01042<br>0.01042|
|**Post-acute IS**|0.00356<br>0.00356|
|**Acute ICH**|0<br>0.00017|
|**Post-acute ICH**|0<br>0.00017|



3. Null CV death probabilities after a first event would be imputed the maximum of all probabilities (i.e. 0.11111) 

||**Mortality risk after two events**|
|---|---|
||**Base case**<br>**Null transition imputed**|
|**Acute MI**|0.11111<br>0.11111|
|**Post-acute MI**|0<br>0.11111|
|**Acute IS**|0<br>0.11111|
|**Post-acute IS**|0<br>0.11111|
|**Acute ICH**|0<br>0.11111|
|**Post-acute ICH**|0<br>0.11111|



5 



<!-- page 314 -->

- Treatment interruption included An interruption of one year after MI is implemented in order to allow patients to take dual antiplatelet therapy (TIC+ASA). Additionally, a three-month interruption after ICH and a onemonth interruption after major bleed are implemented. After an ICH and a major bleed, the treatment cost is omitted during the interruption. No impact on transition probabilities is considered, as per the use of the ITT data set to calculate HRs. 

- Utility values in the event free state are adjusted based on EQ5D UK norms. A multiplicative approach is taken to calculate utility value of subsequent events. 

- Monitoring cost in the event free state is included based on TA317, inflated to 2017/2018 pounds £167.66. 

6

## **Questions for engagement** 

**Issue 1: Should the focus be on the whole population or ‘high risk’ subgroups? The COMPASS trial included patients at elevated cardiovascular risk, based on evidence of peripheral and/or coronary artery disease. Those with coronary artery disease and below 65 yrs of age were required to have additional risk enrichment factors:** • **Age <65 years plus atherosclerosis in ≥2 vascular beds or ≥2 additional risk factors** `o` **Current smoker** `o` **Diabetes mellitus** `o` **Renal dysfunction (eGFR<60 ml/min)** `o` **Heart failure** Should rivaroxaban be considered for the whole population for whom it is licensed, or only for people `o` **Non-lacunar ischaemic stroke** considered to be at higher risk of ischaemic events? **≥1 month ago The results of the COMPASS trial demonstrate consistent benefits for the rivaroxaban + aspirin strategy across the full included population. No heterogeneity of effects is seen. Thus the evidence supports treatment for the whole population in line with the enrichment criteria. Clinicians will judge the combination of risk features, and bleeding risks to decide on whether the COMPASS regimen is appropriate. I do not believe that it should be restricted to the heart failure, poor renal function or disease in 2 vascular beds as patients with other risk features have similar benefits, For example those with previous MI, those with prior (non-lacunar) ischaemic stroke and those with diabetes.** 

**In other related settings (PEGASUS regimen of ticagrelor 1 to 3 years after MI, plus aspirin)** 

Technical engagement response form Rivaroxaban for preventing major cardiovascular events in people with coronary or peripheral artery disease [ID1397] 

3 of 7 



<!-- page 375 -->

**==> picture [268 x 48] intentionally omitted <==**

**clinicians in the NHS have chosen to implement this therapy in only a selected small proportion of the potentially eligible population. This suggests that clinicians will likely also weigh up risks and benefits across the COMPASS eligible population and choose patients to treat based on a combination of risk factors (ie not necessarily only those proposed by the company).** 

Are the subgroups presented by the company clinically relevant and do they represent a population at high baseline risk of thrombotic events as suggested by the company? 

**As indicated above, those patients with peripheral artery disease and those patients with coronary artery disease and markers of higher risk (see above) show clear and consistent evidence of benefit. No heterogeneity of effects of the COMPASS regimen was seen. For the reasons listed above I do not believe that it is appropriate to restrict use to only the subgroups (CAD + PAD or CAD plus poor renal function or heart failure) as other higher risk groups were included in the COMPASS inclusion criteria and demonstrated similar benefit. Clinicians are likely to weigh up all the risk factors that were enrichment factors in COMPASS, and the bleeding risks, before making their recommendations in discussion with the patient.** 

If high risk subgroups are appropriate, should treatment effects be based on the hazard ratios for the whole population, or is it appropriate to accept different treatment effects in the different groups? 

**In my view the treatment should not be restricted to the 3 "high risk subgroups" identified by the company as consistency of treatment effects was demonstrated across the included population without evidence of heterogeneity. I believe that clinicians can weigh up risks and potential benefits in deciding who should be treated, as they currently do for prolonged dual anti-platelet therapy (for example the PEGASUS regimen beyond a year after MI).** 

**Issue 2: Exclusion of clopidogrel as a comparator for people with PAD** 

Is it reasonable to exclude clopidogrel as a relevant comparator for the overall COMPASS trial population and the subgroup of people with CAD and PAD? 

**Clopidogrel has not been tested in the COMPASS trial. As all the guidelines (UK and ESC) recommend aspirin as the antiplatelet agent for patients with chronic CAD, clopidogrel is only recommended in chronic CAD patients who cannot tolerate aspirin (for example due to true hypersensitivity to aspirin). Therefore such patients intolerant of aspirin would not be eligible for the COMPASS regimen. I believe that this indicates that clopidogrel is not a**

By comparison, the** treat people with stable CAD and/or PAD at high risk **rivaroxaban plus aspirin treatment in COMPASS shows a substantially greater 28% relative** of ischaemic events or the subgroup of people with **(2% absolute) risk reduction (unlike the CAPRIE trial, in COMPASS this was on top of well** CAD and PAD? **treated secondary prevention) and it also reduced acute limb events and amputations. (Anand et al Lancet 2017). Thus for patients with CAD plus PAD, for whom aspirin was the prior anti-platelet of choice, then the COMPASS regimen is a relevant option.**

Is the (modified) ISTH classification used in clinical practice in the NHS to define major bleeds? 

**therapy during the first year after MI (they were not naive to dual anti-platelet therapy and hence those with bleeding complications during the first year after MI would not likely be included in PEGASUS). The median time since MI (in those that had prior MI) in COMPASS was 7 years so the situation was different and patients were on just aspirin at baseline. If they required dual anti-platelet therapy, or if they were on an antiplatelet other than aspirin they were not eligible for COMPASS (Canadian Journal of Cardiology 33 (2017) 1027e1035). The modified ISTH classification is not widely used in the NHS. This modified ISTH classification for major bleeds is broader than the original ISTH definition as it includes patients that attended a clinical facility for a bleed but could be discharged without hospital admission and without transfusion or surgical procedures. Thus with rivaroxaban plus aspirin there were 3.1% major bleeds with the modified ISTH definition but only 2.3% with the original ISTH definition (Eikelboom et al 2019 under review)** 

Are the ISTH and TIMI classification methods used to define major bleeds sufficiently similar and able to identify the same number of major bleeds in the same population? 

**No, ISTH and TIMI major bleeds are not sufficiently similar. TIMI major bleeds (these have more severe BARC criteria) were associated with increased one year mortality whereas ISTH bleeds were not (Published by the Academic Research Consortium J Am Coll Cardiol. 2014 May 13;63(18):1866-75).**

|Should rivaroxaban be considered for the whole<br>population for whom it is licensed, or only for people<br>considered to be at higher risk of ischaemic events?|**Strict adherence to the evidence would mean it should be considered for the entire**<br>**population. Other groups that are of high thrombotic risk include**<br>**patients with previous MI / stroke / TIA, multi-vessel coronary disease or extensive**<br>**stenting. |Are the subgroups presented by the company<br>clinically relevant and do they represent a population<br>at high baseline risk of thrombotic events as<br>suggested bythe company?|**Yes, the 3 subgroups identified; CAD plus PAD/HF/PRF are clinically relevant and**<br>**represent patients who are at higher risk of thrombotic events.**|
|If high risk subgroups are appropriate, should<br>treatment effects be based on the hazard ratios for<br>the whole population, or is it appropriate to accept<br>different treatment effects in the different groups?|**The appraisal should consider the entire ITT population as presented in the publication,**<br>**however it is reasonable to take into account the treatment effects of each subgroup. These include patients with aspirin allergies or intolerances, it may also**<br>**be given, instead of aspirin, in patients with concomitant stroke / TIA or PAD.**|
|**Issue 3: Comparison with ticagrelor + aspirin in people with history of MI**||
|Is the subgroup of people with MI clinically relevant<br>and important?|**Yes, this is a very relevant subgroup. |Would limiting the COMPASS trial population to<br>people with a history of MI to align with the<br>PEGASUS trial population reduce the uncertainty in<br>the treatment effect of rivaroxaban+ aspirin when<br>comparing to ticagrelor + aspirin?|**It would certainly help to reduce (but not eliminate) the heterogeneity, especially since only**<br>**approximately 60% of COMPASS patients had a history of MI, with the majority being more**<br>**than 2 years old (approx. 30% at least 5 years old). ## **Issue 4: Transition probabilities for main events (MI, stroke, CV death) in the economic model** 

What is the preferred source for calculating the N/A probability of experiencing a main event in each cycle of the model when there are no events recorded in the COMPASS trial? Would probabilities calculated using REACH registry data be more appropriate or imputing non-zero values from transition probabilities from other health states (ERG preferred method)? **Issue 5:** As each death rate only has a fraction of the CV N/A deaths, is it reasonable to vary the HR for all CV deaths in the DSA to adequately capture the uncertainty around this parameter?

## **1. Introduction** 

This document is the ERG’s review and critique of the response by the company (Bayer Plc Ltd) to technical report issued by NICE to experts on 5[th] April 2019. The ERG received the company’s response on 13[th] May 2019. 

The company updated their analyses in respect of some of the questions for engagement below, with tabulated results in their response document and separate appendix. The company also provided an accompanying updated economic model. 

Below we take each of the key issues for consideration and comment on the company’s response to them. 

## **2. Key issues for consideration** 

**Issue 1 – Should the focus be on the whole population or ‘high risk’ subgroups?** 

|**Questions for engagement**|**ERG comments**|
|---|---|
|a. Should rivaroxaban be<br>considered for the whole<br>population for whom it is<br>licensed, or only for people<br>considered to be at higher risk<br>of ischaemic events?|The company maintain their position, citing feedback<br>from the medical community that prescribing would be<br>focused on patients at the greatest risk of ischaemic<br>events. The company believes that the large majority of<br>patients who would be considered for treatment with<br>rivaroxaban are captured within their submission.<br>Comments from other experts responding to the<br>technical engagement suggest that there may be other<br>high risk patient subgroups for whom treatment would<br>be considered (e.g. those with previous MI; those with<br>diabetes). They suggested that clinicians are likely to<br>choose patients to treat based on a combination of the<br>individual patient’s risk factors (not necessarily only<br>those chosen bythe company).|
|b. Are the subgroups presented<br>by the company clinically<br>relevant and do they represent a<br>population at high baseline risk<br>of thrombotic events as<br>suggested by the company?|The company maintains their rationale for the clinical<br>relevance of the three subgroups, citing literature to<br>support their position (e.g. from the REACH registry).<br>Comments from other experts responding to the<br>technical engagement generally support the clinical<br>relevance of the three subgroups chosen by the<br>company, but note that there are other high risk patient<br>subgroups who would derive similar benefit from<br>rivaroxaban and aspirin.|



2 



<!-- page 396 -->

|c. If high risk subgroups are<br>appropriate, should treatment<br>effects be based on the hazard<br>ratios for the whole population,<br>or is it appropriate to accept<br>different treatment effects in the<br>different groups?|The company considers use of subgroup-specific HRs<br>appropriate as this is based on empirical data from the<br>COMPASS trial. As requested during the technical<br>engagement teleconference, the company has<br>produced results using a “fixed hazard ratio (HR)<br>approach”, i.e. using the HR from the total COMPASS<br>trial population for the subgroup analyses. These<br>analyses incorporate all the ERG preferences<br>(Appendix 1 of the of the company’s Technical<br>engagement response form). The analyses are shown<br>before and after correcting a coding error in of the<br>company’s Technical engagement response form<br>(results with coding error in Tables 1 to 4; corrected<br>results Tables 5 to 8).<br>The ERG has checked and verified the analyses and<br>the coding error. The coding error resulted in negative<br>transition probabilities and the ERG agrees with the<br>correction made by the company. The ERG notes the<br>following with regard to the analyses:<br>•<br>In tables 5-8 of the company’s Technical<br>engagement response form, the total QALYs for<br>ticagrelor + aspirin and rivaroxaban + aspirin<br>have been transposed.<br>•<br>The company has only used fixed HRs for the<br>rivaroxaban + aspirin arm. For the ticagrelor +<br>aspirin arm, HRs for the three respective<br>subgroups of the PEGASUS trial are used. For<br>consistency, the ERG considers that fixed HRs<br>should also be used for the ticagrelor + aspirin<br>arm (i.e. the HR for the whole PEGASUS trial<br>population). We present analyses using fixed<br>HRs for rivaroxaban + aspirin (COMPASS trial)<br>and the ticagrelor + aspirin arm (PEGASUS<br>trial) in Tables 1-3 below. These changes only<br>alter the results for ticagrelor + aspirin.<br>The ERG notes that the results are similar for the<br>HR fixed analyses to those with subgroup specific<br>HR analyses.|
|---|---|



3

## **Issue 2 – Exclusion of clopidogrel as a comparator for people with PAD** 

|**Questions for engagement**|**ERG comments**|
|---|---|
|a. Is it reasonable to exclude<br>clopidogrel as a relevant comparator for<br>the overall COMPASS trial population<br>and the subgroup of people with CAD<br>and PAD?|The company cites NICE clinical guidelines<br>which recommend long-term maintenance with<br>aspirin for secondary prevention of<br>cardiovascular disease. Clopidogrel is only<br>recommended if aspirin is contraindicated or<br>there is hypersensitivity to aspirin. If<br>rivaroxaban is to be added to aspirin then<br>clopidogrel cannot be considered as a<br>comparator.|
|b. Is clopidogrel used in clinical practice<br>in the NHS to treat people with stable<br>CAD and/or PAD at high risk of<br>ischaemic events or the subgroup of<br>people with CAD and PAD?|The company states that clopidogrel<br>monotherapy is used in stable CAD patients in<br>the NHS who are unable to take aspirin.<br>Comments from other experts responding to the<br>technical engagement suggest that clopidogrel<br>is used in patients with CAD and PAD, and PAD<br>alone.|



**Issue 3 – Comparison with ticagrelor + aspirin in people with history of MI** 

|**Questions for engagement**|**ERG comments**|
|---|---|
|a. Is the subgroup of people with MI<br>clinically relevant and important?|The company acknowledges that patients with<br>MI are clinically relevant but, based on medical<br>advice, history of MI alone (i.e. without other<br>risk factors) is insufficient to warrant an<br>additional treatment to aspirin such as<br>rivaroxaban.|
|b. Would limiting the COMPASS trial<br>population to people with a history of MI<br>to align with the PEGASUS trial<br>population reduce the uncertainty in the<br>treatment effect of rivaroxaban+ aspirin<br>when comparing to ticagrelor + aspirin?|The company reiterates their rationale for not<br>including people with a history of MI as a<br>subgroup. Namely, absence/presence of MI<br>was not effect-modifying for the primary<br>outcome of the COMPASS trial; and the<br>requirement for a recent MI is a feature of the<br>marketing authorisation for ticagrelor, but not<br>rivaroxaban, thus they consider ticagrelor to be<br>a “minor comparator”. The company also points<br>out that using MI subgroup-specific HRs<br>conflicts with the recommendation to use fixed<br>(whole trial) HRs for subgroup analyses (see<br>Issue 1). Furthermore, such a subgroup<br>analysis would be post-hoc and based on a|



5 



<!-- page 399 -->

||subset of patients who had a previous MI_and_<br>restricted to MI in the last three years. The ERG<br>acknowledges this as a limitation, and thus any<br>MI subgroup analysis should only be<br>considered to be illustrative rather than<br>confirmatory.<br>Comments from other experts responding to the<br>technical engagement note the heterogeneity<br>between patients in the COMPASS and<br>PEGASUS trials (in terms of prior treatment<br>history in those with a previous MI). This<br>heterogeneity makes comparisons between the<br>two trials problematic.<br>Despite the above reservations. The company<br>has provided analyses using a subgroup<br>restricted to patients having had an MI one to<br>three years prior to enrolment in the trial. The<br>company has produced two analyses: MI<br>subgroup-specific HRs (Table 9) and fixed HRs<br>(from the whole COMPASS population) using<br>transition probabilities for the subgroup who had<br>a previous MI (Table 10). The ICERs in Table 9<br>are slightly higher than the ERG MI subgroup<br>scenario analysis previously reported in the<br>ERG report (Table 75), and the results in Table<br>10 are similar to those produced for fixed HRs<br>(Table 5 – corrected company base case). The<br>results of the company’s analyses show that the<br>ICERs for rivaroxaban and aspirin remained<br>below £20,000 per QALY gained for the recent<br>MI patient subgroup.<br>The ERG has attempted to reproduce these<br>analyses using the transition probabilities in<br>Appendix 5 of the of the company’s Technical<br>engagement response form and hazard ratios in<br>Appendix 4 but is unable to replicate the results<br>shown in Table 9 and 10 of the company’s<br>Technical engagement response form.|
|---|---|
|c. Is the (modified) ISTH classification<br>used in clinical practice in the NHS to<br>define major bleeds?|The company states that this classification<br>score is used in clinical trials but not used in<br>clinical practice. This concurs with comments<br>from other experts responding to the technical|



6 



<!-- page 400 -->

||engagement and expert clinical advice to the<br>ERG(stated in the ERG report).|
|---|---|
|d. Are the ISTH and TIMI classification<br>methods used to define major bleeds<br>sufficiently similar and able to identify<br>the same number of major bleeds in the<br>same population?|The company notes (as per their original<br>submission to NICE) that the modified ISTH<br>criteria has a broader definition of major bleeds<br>compared to TIMI and therefore the ISTH would<br>classify more bleeds as major than TIMI. This<br>reflects the views of the experts responding to<br>the technical engagement. The company also<br>states that they are not aware of any evidence<br>that the different classification schemes used in<br>the COMPASS trial and the PEGASUS trial<br>(modified ISTH and TIMI, respectively) would<br>affect the respective hazard ratios versus<br>aspirin (which are used in the economic model).|



**Issue 4 – Transition probabilities for main events (MI, stroke, CV death) in the** 

**economic model** 

|**Questions for engagement**|**ERG comments**|
|---|---|
|a. What is the preferred source for<br>calculating the probability of<br>experiencing a main event in each<br>cycle of the model when there are no<br>events recorded in the COMPASS trial?<br>Would probabilities calculated using<br>REACH registry data be more<br>appropriate or imputing non-zero values<br>from transition probabilities from other<br>health states (ERG preferred method)?|The company agrees that it is more clinically<br>plausible to substitute zero transition<br>probabilities with non-zero values. The<br>company have adjusted the transition<br>probabilities from other sources (i.e. the<br>REACH registry).<br>The ERG has checked the company analyses<br>in Tables 11-14 of the company’s Technical<br>engagement response form. The ERG was not<br>able to replicate these results exactly but<br>obtained very similar results. Including the<br>adjustment from the REACH registry for some<br>of the transition probabilities does not<br>significantly change the results for the whole<br>COMPASS population but the results for the<br>subgroups become more cost-effective for<br>rivaroxaban + aspirin by about £2-3,000 per<br>QALY. The ERG agrees with the approach<br>taken by the company and considers that<br>adjusting transition probabilities according to<br>the HR observed in the REACH registry is<br>appropriate.|

## NATIONAL INSTITUTE FOR HEALTH AND CARE EXCELLENCE 

## **Technical report-updated after technical engagement** 

## **Rivaroxaban for preventing atherothrombotic events in people with coronary or peripheral artery disease** 

## **1. Summary of the post-engagement technical report** 

- 1.1 This document is the post-engagement version of the technical report for this appraisal. It has been prepared by the **technical team** with input from the lead team and chair of the appraisal committee. 

The post-engagement technical report issued by the appraisal committee to help it make decisions at the appraisal committee meeting. A draft version of this technical report was sent out for consultation between 11th March and 8th April 2019. The draft report included a list of issues that have an impact on the certainty of the company’s estimates of clinical or cost effectiveness. The aim of the consultation was to seek feedback from consultees and commentators on these issues to help inform the technical team’s preferred modelling assumptions. 

The aim of the post-engagement version of the technical report is to: 

- summarise the feedback that was received on the issues that were identified originally 

- explain how the feedback has or has not been helpful in resolving areas of uncertainty 

Usually, only unresolved or uncertain key issues will be discussed at the appraisal committee meeting. 



<!-- page 403 -->

The technical report includes: 

- a commentary on the evidence received and written statements 

- technical judgements on the evidence by the **technical team** 

- reflections on NICE’s structured decision-making framework. 

This report is based on: 

- the evidence and views submitted by the **company** , consultees and their nominated clinical experts and patient experts and 

- the evidence review group ( **ERG** ) report. 

The technical report should be read with the full supporting documents for this appraisal. 

- 1.2 After technical engagement the technical team has collated the comments received and, if relevant, updated the scientific judgement by the technical team and rationale. The issues that were considered at technical engagement are described in detail in section 2 below, along with the feedback that was received. The following table summarizes the current status of each issue in terms of the technical team’s view on the level of outstanding uncertainty.

An ERG scenario<br>analysis shows that the model results are<br>more sensitive to changes in the all CV<br>death HR than shown in the company<br>deterministic sensitivity analysis (DSA).<br>The same issue has led to an<br>underestimation of parameter uncertainty<br>in the PSA. The technical team consider<br>that the correct approach is to vary the HR<br>for “all CV deaths” as in the ERG scenario<br>analysis|The company notes that the ERG<br>approach implicitly assumes each<br>component is independent of the others<br>whereas varying all the components<br>together assumes that they are perfectly<br>correlated.  It is likely that neither<br>approach is entirely realistic, and the ERG<br>approach may overestimate uncertainty.<br>Therefore, it should be viewed as a worst-<br>case view of uncertainty and both results<br>by the company and ERG be considered<br>by the committee. ## **2. Key issues for consideration** 

## _**Issue 1 – Should the focus be on the whole population or ‘high risk’ subgroups?**_ 

|**Background/description of**<br>**issue**|-<br>Rivaroxaban, co-administered with aspirin, has a marketing authorisation “for the prevention of<br>atherothrombotic events in adult patients with coronary artery disease (CAD) or symptomatic peripheral<br>artery disease (PAD) at high risk of ischaemic events”.<br>-<br>The**company**presents evidence for the overall licensed population (people with stable CAD and/or PAD<br>at high risk of ischaemic events) but the submission focuses on 3 subpopulations as follows:<br>**i.**<br>patients with CAD and PAD<br>**ii.**<br>patients with CAD and HF<br>**iii.**<br>patients with CAD and poor renal function (PRF) with eGFR<60ml/min<br>-<br>The NICE scope stated that “If the evidence allows subgroups defined as having a higher risk of a<br>major cardiovascular event will be considered”<br>-<br>Only one of the subpopulations was pre-specified as a subgroup of interest in the COMPASS trial and<br>none of the statistical tests for interaction was significant. Therefore, it might be more appropriate to use<br>the whole trial estimates of hazard ratio for estimating treatment effects in subgroups.|
|---|---|
|**Why this issue is important**|It is important to know if the subgroups presented by the company are clinically relevant. Do they represent<br>the people at highest risk of ischaemic events?  It is also important to consider how treatment-effects should<br>be estimated for subgroups.|
|**Questions for engagement**|a. Should rivaroxaban be considered for the whole population for whom it is licensed, or only for people<br>considered to be at higher risk of ischaemic events?<br>b. Are the subgroups presented by the company clinically relevant and do they represent a population at<br>high baseline risk of thrombotic events as suggested by the company?|





<!-- page 408 -->

||c. If high risk subgroups are appropriate, should treatment effects be based on the hazard ratios for the<br>whole population, or is it appropriate to accept different treatment effects in the different groups?|
|---|---|
|**Technical team preliminary**<br>**scientific judgement and**<br>**rationale**|-<br>The**technical team**would welcome feedback on whether the appraisal should focus on the whole<br>population with CAD or symptomatic peripheral artery disease (PAD) at high risk of ischaemic events (the<br>marketing authorisation).<br>-<br>The**company**argues that the 3 subgroups they have presented are at higher baseline risk of thrombotic<br>events and stand to benefit the most from treatment with rivaroxaban. Clinical expert opinion on the<br>validity of this statement would be appreciated.<br>-<br>The**technical team**is not convinced that there is evidence of between-subgroup heterogeneity in relative<br>treatment effects and that, therefore, hazard ratios for the whole ITT population should be applied to the<br>‘higher-risk’ subgroups.|
|**Summary of comments**|Comments from experts:<br>-<br>The subgroups identified by the company are clinically relevant and do represent people who are at<br>greater risk of thrombotic events. Although it would be reasonable to limit the use of rivaroxaban to<br>only these subgroups of people that are at high risk of ischaemic events, there is no between group<br>heterogeneity in treatment effects, therefore the overall population treatment effects should be applied.<br>Furthermore, in order to ensure consistency in approach and to reduce uncertainty arising from using<br>results from subgroups which are small, treatment with rivaroxaban should not be restricted to the 3<br>high risk subgroups as there is no between group heterogeneity.<br>-<br>Clinicians in the NHS have been prescribing ticagrelor+ aspirin in a smaller and select population than<br>the population recommended by NICE. This suggests that clinicians will also likely select patients to<br>treat with rivaroxaban + aspirin based on a combination of individual patient risk factors and weigh up<br>the bleeding risks and treatment benefits for the whole COMPASS population, rather than considering<br>only the subgroups proposed by the company.<br>-<br>Other groups that are of high thrombotic risk include people with previous MI / stroke, multi-vessel<br>coronary disease, extensive stenting and diabetes. People with risk factors other than the 3 subgroups<br>proposed by the company were included in the COMPASS trial and showed similar treatment benefits<br>Comments from professional organisations:<br>-<br>The whole population should be considered to ensure consistency of approach and to reduce<br>uncertainty.|





<!-- page 409 -->

||Comments from company:<br>-<br>Rationale for the 3 subgroups proposed is based on feedback from clinical experts that prescribing<br>would be focused on patients at the greatest risk of ischaemic events. Literature (for example, from the<br>REACH registry) also supports the clinical relevance of the subgroups. Therefore, although there may<br>be some people with baseline risks that may not be captured within the 3 subgroups, most patients<br>who would be considered for treatment with rivaroxaban would be captured within these<br>subpopulations.<br>-<br>Subgroup-specific hazard ratios (HRs) are appropriate as this is based on observed data from the<br>COMPASS trial. However, to reduce uncertainty, analyses using a “fixed HR approach, that is, using<br>the HR from the overall COMPASS population were presented (also taking into account a coding error<br>which resulted in negative transition probabilities). The analyses showed that using a fixed HR does<br>not produce ICER’s in excess of £20,000 per QALY gained.<br>Critique from the ERG:<br>-<br>After checking and verifying the analyses by the company shown in table 1-4 above and the coding<br>error, the ERG agrees with the correction made by the company. However, they note:<br>`o`The total QALYs for ticagrelor + aspirin and rivaroxaban + aspirin have been transposed in the<br>updated cost-effectiveness results tables provided by the company.<br>`o`The company has only used fixed HRs for the rivaroxaban + aspirin arm in the updated<br>analyses provided. For the ticagrelor + aspirin arm, HRs for the three respective subgroups of<br>the PEGASUS trial are used. The ERG considers that fixed HRs should also be used for the<br>ticagrelor + aspirin arm (that is, the HR for the whole PEGASUS trial population) for<br>consistency. Analyses using fixed HRs for rivaroxaban + aspirin (COMPASS trial) and the<br>ticagrelor + aspirin arm (PEGASUS trial) show that using fixed HRs only alter the results for<br>ticagrelor + aspirin.|
|---|---|
|**Technical team scientific**<br>**judgement after engagement**|-<br>The experts responding to technical engagement noted that in order to reduce uncertainty and ensure<br>consistency of approach, the whole COMPASS population should be considered rather than the 3<br>subpopulations put forward by the company. Expert opinion suggests that there may be high-risk groups<br>other than those presented by the company for whom treatment would be considered (for example, those<br>with previous MI; those with diabetes) that would derive similar benefit from rivaroxaban and aspirin.<br>Additionally, there is little evidence to suggest heterogeneity of treatment effect between the subgroups.|





<!-- page 410 -->

## _**Issue 2 – Exclusion of clopidogrel as a comparator for people with PAD**_ 

|**Background/description of**<br>**issue**|-<br>The**company**does not present clinical and cost-effectiveness results versus clopidogrel for the<br>subpopulation of people with combined CAD/PAD, or the overall COMPASS population, despite the NICE<br>scope specifying that clopidogrel is a comparator for people with PAD.<br>-<br>The**company**omitted clopidogrel from its systematic review and did not present any clinical or cost-<br>effectiveness evidence for it. The ERG notes that the omission of clopidogrel may be tied to the fact that<br>the company is not seeking a recommendation for the PAD only population in their CS. Clinical expert advice<br>to the**ERG**shows that clopidogrel is used to treat people with stable CAD and PAD.|
|---|---|
|**Why this issue is important**|Although clopidogrel is not relevant for the CAD and HF or CAD and PRF subgroups, it might be considered<br>relevant for the overall COMPASS trial population and for the CAD and PAD subgroup|
|**Questions for engagement**|a. Is it reasonable to exclude clopidogrel as a relevant comparator for the overall COMPASS trial population<br>and the subgroup of people with CAD and PAD?<br>b. Is clopidogrel used in clinical practice in the NHS to treat people with stable CAD and/or PAD at high risk<br>of ischaemic events or the subgroup of people with CAD and PAD?|
|**Technical team preliminary**<br>**scientific judgement and**<br>**rationale**|The**technical team**is concerned that omitting clopidogrel from analyses will exclude valid comparisons of<br>rivaroxaban + aspirin with clopidogrel in clinically relevant groups of people. The company states that it is not<br>seeking a recommendation for the PAD only population. As a consequence, the decision problem set out in<br>the scope is not adequately addressed. Clinical opinion would be valued to determine if clopidogrel is a valid<br>comparator for people with stable CAD and/or PAD at high risk of ischaemic events and subgroup of people<br>with CAD and PAD.|





<!-- page 411 -->

|**Summary of comments**|Comments from experts:<br>-<br>One clinical expert considers that clopidogrel should be included as a comparator for the overall<br>COMPASS population (adult patients with CAD or symptomatic PAD at high risk of ischaemic events)<br>as it is frequently used in this population.  It is the current anti-platelet treatment of choice for people<br>with PAD and is also used to treat certain subgroups of people with CAD such as people with aspirin<br>allergies or intolerances. Clopidogrel may also be given instead of aspirin, in patients with concomitant<br>stroke / transient ischaemic attack or PAD.<br>-<br>Another expert does not consider clopidogrel to be a comparator for the overall COMPASS population<br>as clopidogrel is only recommended in chronic CAD patients who cannot tolerate aspirin. People<br>intolerant of aspirin were not be eligible for the COMPASS trial which indicates that clopidogrel is not a<br>relevant comparator for the overall COMPASS population.<br>-<br>Clopidogrel is not often used long-term for patients with CAD only. It is commonly used in those with<br>CAD and PAD and those with PAD alone. In the CAD+PAD subgroup, clopidogrel is an obvious<br>comparator.<br>Comments from company:<br>-<br>NICE guidelines recommend people with stable CAD be maintained long-term with aspirin 75mg daily<br>for secondary prevention of cardiovascular disease. In these guidelines clopidogrel is only<br>recommended if aspirin is contraindicated or if there is hypersensitivity to aspirin.  Rivaroxaban cannot<br>be added to aspirin if the patient has a contraindication or hypersensitivity which means that<br>clopidogrel is only an option where rivaroxaban is not – it therefore cannot be considered a<br>comparator.<br>-<br>In the acute setting, patients with CAD may be treated with clopidogrel and aspirin (people with stable<br>CAD treated in the long term are not). Rivaroxaban + aspirin is not being used in acute patients,<br>therefore clopidogrel is not a valid comparator in the long-term.|
|---|---|
|**Technical team scientific**<br>**judgement after engagement**|-<br>Clopidogrel is a valid comparator for people with PAD (with or without concomitant CAD) based on<br>existing NICE guidance. This is in line with the scope for this appraisal. One expert suggests that the<br>benefit of clopidogrel over aspirin is modest in this population.<br>-<br>Clopidogrel is not a valid comparator for the overall COMPASS population because aspirin remains<br>the antiplatelet of choice in people with stable CAD. Clopidogrel is only recommended where aspirin is<br>unsuitable.|





<!-- page 412 -->

## _**Issue 3 – Comparison with ticagrelor + aspirin in people with history of MI**_ 

- **Background/description of** - To estimate the relative efficacy of rivaroxaban + aspirin compared with ticagrelor + aspirin, the company **issue** carried out an indirect treatment comparison (ITC). The 2 trials included were COMPASS and PEGASUS. The **ERG** notes that although the ITC is methodologically sound, there are some important differences between the patients enrolled in the 2 trials: 

      - the proportion of patients with a prior MI was 100% in PEGASUS but 62% in COMPASS 

      - time elapsed since an MI was restricted to between 1-3 years in PEGASUS whereas patients could have had an MI at any time within the past 20 years in COMPASS 

      - the proportion of patients with PAD was only 5% in PEGASUS but 27% in COMPASS 

      - `o` The primary safety outcome (major bleeding) was defined by the TIMI criteria in PEGASUS but modified ISTH criteria in COMPASS 

      - `o` PEGASUS included sudden unexpected cardiac deaths in the definition of a MI whereas COMPASS excluded (assessed as a CV-related death instead) 

   - The company did not use the results of the ITC in their economic model. Instead HRs from the COMPASS and PEGASUS trials were applied to the transition probabilities for the aspirin only group to calculate transition probabilities for rivaroxaban + aspirin and ticagrelor + aspirin. 

   - - NICE guidance TA420 states that ticagrelor is an option for preventing atherothrombotic events in adults who had a MI and who are at high risk of a further event. Thus, for the approximately 38% of patients in the COMPASS trial, who had not experienced a previous MI, ticagrelor is not a relevant comparator.  This number may be even smaller as the proportion in the 38% who would have had an MI in the last 2 years is unknown. 

   - - Most of the differences in the COMPASS and PEGASUS population relate to the proportion of people with an MI. When queried why the COMPASS trial population in the ITC was not limited to the subgroup with a history of MI, the company said that adjusting the COMPASS population to a subgroup with a history of MI was not necessary because having a 'history of MI' is not effect-modifying for the primary efficacy composite outcome 

   - - A scenario analysis by the **ERG** comparing rivaroxaban + aspirin and ticagrelor + aspirin for the subgroup of patients with a prior MI showed rivaroxaban + aspirin is more cost-effective than the company’s base case. The **ERG** used HRs for MI, stroke and CV death from subgroup analyses in patients with a previous - 

   - MI from COMPASS and transition probabilities derived from PEGASUS for MI (for the event free group). 



<!-- page 413 -->

||The time period for an MI was much longer in COMPASS than in PEGASUS (up to 20 years and 1-3<br>years, respectively) and the HRs are not restricted to those who had a MI in the last two years in<br>COMPASS (as is the case for people in PEGASUS) so comparison is only illustrative. (see ERG report<br>section 4.4.1.2.1 for more information).<br>-<br>Similarly, a subgroup analysis of people with CAD in COMPASS with an MI in the previous two years<br>showed a more favourable effect on the primary composite efficacy outcome (cardiovascular death, stroke<br>or MI) compared to patients whose previous MI occurred longer ago. The**ERG**speculates that if HRs for<br>patients with an MI in the previous two years were available for all outcomes, the cost-effectiveness<br>results are likely to be more favourable to rivaroxaban than in the**ERG’**s scenario analysis. However,<br>given the lack of evidence of heterogeneity in treatment effects, it may be inappropriate to use subgroup<br>specific hazard ratios for these analyses (see issue 1 above).|
|---|---|
|**Why this issue is important**|-<br>“People who have had a previous MI” is a subgroup of interest listed in the NICE scope and expert clinical<br>advice to the ERG suggests patients with a prior MI are at risk of recurrent MIs/other events.<br>-<br>A scenario analysis by the ERG shows that rivaroxaban + aspirin may potentially be more cost-effective<br>than the company’s base case for the subgroup of patients with a prior MI.|
|**Questions for engagement**|a. Is the subgroup of people with MI clinically relevant and important?<br>b.Would limiting the COMPASS trial population to people with a history of MI to align with the PEGASUS<br>trial population reduce the uncertainty in the treatment effect of rivaroxaban+ aspirin when comparing<br>to ticagrelor + aspirin?<br>c. Is the (modified) ISTH classification used in clinical practice in the NHS to define major bleeds?<br>d. Are the ISTH and TIMI classification methods used to define major bleeds sufficiently similar and able<br>to identify the same number of major bleeds in the same population?|
|**Technical team preliminary**<br>**scientific judgement and**<br>**rationale**|The**technical team**is of the view that analyses limiting the COMPASS population to people with a previous<br>MI (and those with an MI within the previous two years) should be explored by the company in order to align<br>the COMPASS population to that of PEGASUS, all of whom had a previous MI.|
|**Summary of comments**|Comments from experts:<br>-<br>One expert notes that as the company is seeking a recommendation for a population of people with<br>primarily CAD at high risk of ischaemic events, people with a history of MI represent a subgroup that may<br>arguably be at highest risk. Limiting the COMPASS trial population to people with a history of MI to align<br>with the PEGASUS trial population would help to reduce the heterogeneity in the 2 trials.|





<!-- page 414 -->

- In contrast, another expert noted that COMPASS was not designed as a post-MI trial and it would not be appropriate to align it with the PEGASUS population as 100% of patients in PEGASUS had an MI  in the previous 1 to 3 years and had had treatment with a P2Y12 antagonist (usually ticagrelor or clopidogrel) + aspirin for a year whereas only 5% of the COMPASS CAD patients had their MI within the prior year. `o` Patients with an indication for dual antiplatelet therapy were not eligible for inclusion in the COMPASS trial. Therefore. although rivaroxaban +aspirin can be considered as a treatment option after dual anti-platelet therapy, it is not an alternative to dual anti-platelet therapy in the acute setting after an MI. 

`o` It is not appropriate to limit the COMPASS population to align with PEGASUS because there are not only differences in baseline characteristics and duration after MI between the two trial populations, but also because the PEGASUS population was exposed to dual antiplatelet therapy during the first year after MI. This means that patients in PEGASUS were not naive to dual antiplatelet therapy and people with bleeding complications during the first year after MI would not likely have been eligible for inclusion in the trial. In contrast, the median time since MI (in COMPASS patients that had prior MI) was 7 years with patients on long term maintenance treatment with aspirin. The baseline bleeding risk in this population would naturally be higher compared to the PEGASUS population. - The modified ISTH classification criteria used to classify bleeds is not widely used in the NHS and is more sensitive than the TIMI classification criteria for bleeds as it includes patients that attended a clinical facility for a bleed but could be discharged without hospital admission and without transfusion or surgical procedures. Comments from the company: - Although people who have had previous MI are a clinically relevant subgroup, clinical expert advice shows that having a history of MI in isolation (that is, without other factors predisposing the patient to higher baseline risk of events) is insufficient reason alone to warrant the addition of rivaroxaban to ongoing treatment with aspirin. Adjusting the COMPASS population to match PEGASUS is not necessary and increases uncertainty: `o` absence/presence of MI was not effect-modifying for the primary outcome of the COMPASS trial according to a subgroup analysis of the primary efficacy outcome in CAD patients. This showed that there was no significant interaction for the primary outcome according to history of MI. `o` the requirement for a recent MI is a restriction in the marketing authorisation for ticagrelor. Rivaroxaban does not have this restriction and its target population is not defined by having a history of MI 



<!-- page 415 -->

- using MI subgroup-specific HRs conflicts with the preference for fixed (whole trial population) HRs for subgroup analyses (as discussed in Issue 1). 

`o` subgroup analysis would be post-hoc and would involve subdividing the COMPASS population twice to a subset of patients who had a previous MI _and_ restricted to MI in the last three years. This would increase uncertainty in the results rather than decreasing it - Despite these reservations, analyses for the overall COMPASS population restricted to a subgroup of people who had an MI 1-3 years prior to enrolment in the trial are provided (not for the 3 subpopulations proposed by the company). - Results using HRs specific to the CAD + recent MI population and for the CAD + recent MI population but using fixed HRs from the overall COMPASS trial population show that the ICERs for rivaroxaban and aspirin remained below £20,000 per QALY gained for subgroup of people with previous MI. - The TIMI or ISTH bleeding classification scores are not used in clinical practice in the NHS and are tools used in clinical trials for regulatory purposes. The modified ISTH criteria has a broader definition of major bleeds compared to TIMI and would classify more bleeds as major. IMI or ISTH bleeding classification scores are not used in clinical practice in the NHS and are tools used in clinical trials for regulatory purposes. The modified ISTH criteria has a broader definition of major bleeds compared to TIMI and would classify more bleeds as major. - The company also states that they are not aware of any evidence that the different classification scores used in the COMPASS trial and the PEGASUS trial (modified ISTH and TIMI, respectively) would affect the respective hazard ratios versus aspirin. This is important as it is the HRs that are used in the economic model. Critique from the ERG: - The ERG acknowledges that a subgroup analysis restricting the COMPASS population to people with a previous MI would be post-hoc analyses that subdivides the population twice which increases uncertainty in the results. Thus, any MI subgroup analysis should only be considered to be illustrative rather than confirmatory. - The ERG notes that the ICERs in table 8 above are slightly higher than the ERG MI subgroup scenario analysis previously reported in the ERG report (Table 75). The results in table 10 are similar to those produced for fixed HRs (Table 1 – corrected company base case above for the overall COMPASS population). - The ERG was unable to replicate the results of these analyses using data provided by the company 



<!-- page 416 -->

|**Technical team scientific**<br>**judgement after engagement**|-<br>The technical team is of the view that analyses based on limiting the COMPASS trial population to people<br>with a history of MI to align with the PEGASUS trial population are highly uncertain and are not useful for<br>decision making.<br>-<br>Ticagrelor, in combination with aspirin, is recommended as an option for preventing atherothrombotic<br>events in adults who had a MI in the previous 1-2 years. Given that only 5% of the COMPASS trial<br>population with CAD had had their MI within the prior year, ticagrelor is a valid treatment option for only a<br>small proportion of the overall trial population. Taking this as well the uncertainty in the results of the ITC<br>into account, the committee should discuss the relevance of ticagrelor as a valid comparator for the<br>overall COMPASS population and consider whether the key comparator in this appraisal is aspirin.|
|---|---|



## _**Issue 4 – Transition probabilities for main events (MI, stroke, CV death) in the economic model**_ 

|**Background/description of**<br>**issue**|-<br>The transition probabilities (which is the probability of a patient experiencing a main event in each cycle of<br>the model) for the first 4 years of the model are based upon patient-level data from COMPASS and are<br>constant for this period. From the 5th year transition probabilities are informed by data from the REACH<br>registry.<br>-<br>For some transitions from health states other than ‘event-free’, there were no events recorded in<br>COMPASS. When this was the case, the**company**assigned a probability of having such events to zero,<br>that is, it was assumed that the cohort in certain health states was not at risk of experiencing events. The<br>**company**took this approach on the advice of clinical experts but included a scenario analysis in its<br>submission in which zero transitions had been replaced with non-zero values from the event-free<br>probabilities to reflect the real-life risk.<br>-<br>Furthermore, some of the non-zero probabilities were considered unrealistically low. For example, people in<br>the ‘acute MI’ health state had a lower probability of MI than those who were event-free. This seems<br>implausible in the light of clinical advice that people who have had a first MI are at higher risk of a second<br>during the first 3 months after the event.**ERG**considers including zero transition probabilities is unrealistic<br>and notes that for some transitions, the transition probabilities appear counter-intuitive, for example where<br>an individual’s chance of experiencing another MI is lower after experiencing an MI than before<br>experiencing an MI.<br>-<br>The**ERG**prefers imputing non-zero transition probabilities from transition probabilities from other health<br>states and have incorporated this in their base case based on clinical expert advice demonstrating that the<br>risk of another event during the three months after an event is higher than for those in the event-free group.|
|---|---|





<!-- page 417 -->

||Alternatively, the**ERG**suggests that transition probabilities for events where none was recorded in the trial<br>could be calculated using data from another source, such as the REACH registry.<br>-<br>The**ERG**also highlights that as COMPASS was not powered to detect differences between the<br>subpopulations, some of the HRs used to calculate the TPs for main events particularly for the 3<br>subpopulations are highly uncertain with wide 95% confidence intervals (principally for intracranial<br>haemorrhage and fatal bleeding).|
|---|---|
|**Why this issue is important**|The transition probabilities included in the company model are inconsistent and mean that those patients in the<br>acute MI state may have lower probabilities of an event that those in the event-free state. This raises concerns<br>about the face validity of the model as experiencing an event would normally be risk factor for future events.|
|**Questions for engagement**|a. What is the preferred source for calculating the probability of experiencing a main event in each cycle of<br>the model when there are no events recorded in the COMPASS trial? Would probabilities calculated<br>using REACH registry data be more appropriate or imputing non-zero values from transition<br>probabilities from other health states (ERG preferred method)?|
|**Technical team preliminary**<br>**scientific judgement and**<br>**rationale**|-<br>The technical team agree with the ERG that it is implausible to assume zero risks. Some of the transition<br>probabilities appear counter-intuitive, for example where an individual’s chance of experiencing another MI<br>is lower after experiencing an MI than before experiencing an MI.<br>-<br>The technical team would welcome scenario analyses where alternative approaches to calculating TPs for<br>main events (MI, stroke, CV death) are explored. This may include imputing non-zero TPs from TPs from<br>other health states or using other sources such as the REACH registry.|
|**Summary of comments**|Comments from company:<br>-<br>Agree that it is more clinically plausible to substitute zero transition probabilities with non-zero values and<br>present scenario analyses using values for transition probabilities from other sources such as the REACH<br>registry. Results incorporate all ERG preferences and use subgroup specific HRs. These analyses also<br>rectify the coding error identified in issue 1. The scenario analysis shows improved ICERS compared to the<br>base case reported in issue 1:<br>Critique from the ERG:<br>-<br>The ERG checked the company scenario analyses results presented in table 10-13 above and could not<br>replicate the results presented by the company although similar results were obtained.<br>-<br>Including the adjustment from the REACH registry for some of the transition probabilities does not<br>significantly change the results for the whole COMPASS population but the results for the subgroups<br>become more cost-effective for rivaroxaban + aspirin by about £2-3,000 per QALY. ERG agrees that<br>adjusting transition probabilities according to the HR observed in the REACH registry is appropriate.|





<!-- page 418 -->

**Technical team scientific** - The technical team agrees with the approach proposed by the ERG and taken by the company to substitute **judgement after engagement** zero transition probabilities values with non-zero values taken from REACH registry. 

## _**Issue 5 – Underestimation of impact of CV death on the incremental cost effectiveness ratio (ICER)**_ 

|**Background/description of**<br>**issue**||-<br>The**company’s**deterministic sensitivity analyses (DSA) underestimates the impact of varying the stratified<br>mortality outcome of ‘CV death’. This has the largest effect on the ICER in scenario analyses carried out by<br>the**ERG**.<br>-<br>The mortality outcome cardiovascular (CV) death is stratified by death due to MI, stroke, CV procedure,<br>sudden cardiac death, ‘other CV death’ and ‘all CV death’.  However, the HR estimates from COMPASS,<br>are for “all CV death” and the same HR is assumed for all stratified death events in the model as for “all CV<br>death”. In its DSA (and PSA), the**company**has varied each of these mortality HRs separately. As each<br>death rate only has a fraction of the CV deaths, the effect of varying the HRs individually in the DSA and<br>PSA is small.<br>-<br>The**ERG**considers that a better approach is to set all CV mortality HRs to vary together, rather than<br>independently.  In a scenario analysis, the HRs for all CV death were varied whilst assuming the same HRs<br>for all the CV mortality events. The HRs for mortality due to MI, stroke, HF, CV procedure, sudden cardiac<br>death, other CV death and all CV death were set to the lower and higher 95%CI of the HR for all CV death.<br>These results are shown below and show that the model results are more sensitive to changes in the all CV<br>death HR than shown in the company DSA. In<br>contrast, varying all the components together assumes that they are perfectly correlated.  It is likely that<br>neither approach is entirely realistic that is, none of the components are completely independent nor are<br>they perfectly correlated.<br>-<br>It could be argued that the company analysis underestimates uncertainty, however, it is equally valid to<br>argue that the ERG approach overestimates uncertainty. Therefore, the scenario analysis presented by<br>the ERG should be viewed in the context of representing a worst-case view of uncertainty.  The<br>company note that no attempt to provide a ‘middle-ground’ analysis has been taken given the lack of<br>data to estimate the extent of correlation and both the company results and that of the ERG scenario<br>analysis should be considered by the committee.||||||

***** The ERG was not able to replicate this ICER exactly but obtained very similar result. 

****** The ERG noted that the company only applied fixed HRs for the rivaroxaban + aspirin arm of the COMPASS trial when presenting results using a fixed HR approach. For the ticagrelor + aspirin arm, HRs for the three respective subgroups of the PEGASUS trial were used. The ERG considered that fixed HRs should have been used for the ticagrelor + aspirin arm as well (that is, the HR for the whole PEGASUS trial population) for consistency. When the analyses were repeated using fixed HRs for rivaroxaban + aspirin (COMPASS trial) and the ticagrelor + aspirin arm (PEGASUS trial), results for ticagrelor + aspirin only were altered. The ICER for rivaroxaban+ aspirin compared to ticagrelor + aspirin in the CAD + PAD population decreased to £14,878 per 



<!-- page 423 -->

QALY gained with ticagrelor+ aspirin remaining extendedly dominated in the overall COMPASS population and remaining 2 subgroups. 

**Table 2: Outstanding uncertainties in the evidence base** 

|**Area of uncertainty**|**Why this issue is important**|**Likely impact on the cost-effectiveness**<br>**estimate**|
|---|---|---|
|**Different classification criteria for the**<br>**primary safety outcome (major bleeding)**<br>**used in COMPASS and PEGASUS**|Major bleeding was defined according to<br>modified ISTH criteria in COMPASS. The<br>modified ISTH is more sensitive and included<br>any bleeding that led to hospitalisation with<br>or without an overnight stay. The**company**<br>noted that these events may not be<br>considered major bleeds in other<br>antithrombotic trials, and therefore may<br>introduce potential over-reporting of<br>hospitalisations<br>The bleeding classification criteria used in<br>PEGASUS is the Thrombosis in Myocardial<br>Infarction (TIMI) criteria. Clinical expert<br>advice to the**ERG**suggests that although the<br>ISTH is more detailed than the TIMI<br>classification, it is used less frequently in<br>clinical practice in the NHS compared to the<br>TIMI and HAS-BLED classifications.<br>The use of two different classification criteria<br>to define the primary safety outcome of<br>rivaroxaban for the comparison against<br>ticagrelor as the ISTH criteria is more<br>sensitive and can lead to over-reporting of<br>hospitalisation due to local practices,<br>physicians’ experience, and local in-and out-<br>patient policies.|Effect on the cost-effectiveness estimate is<br>unknown|





<!-- page 424 -->

||As major bleeding events in both arms of<br>each trial were categorised using the same<br>bleeding criteria, the relative effect of using 2<br>different criteria’s is unknown and may<br>possibly not be significant.||
|---|---|---|
|**Subgroup analyses**|Subpopulations comprise around 20% of the<br>randomised population and will be<br>statistically underpowered for efficacy and<br>safety outcomes.|Effect on the cost-effectiveness estimate is<br>unknown|
|**Missing data for the subpopulations in the**<br>**PEGASUS trial**|The**company**notes that there are several<br>missing HRs in the PEGASUS trial for the 3<br>subpopulations and states that HRs for the<br>overall PEGASUS trial population were used<br>for these missing inputs. The**ERG**notes that<br>for the subpopulation with CAD+HF there are<br>no HRs available and all have been taken<br>from the overall PEGASUS trial population. It<br>agrees that in the absence of evidence, it is<br>reasonable to assume that for the main<br>events, the HRs would be similar between<br>the main trial population and the<br>subpopulations. The**ERG**further notes that<br>none of the subgroups were significantly<br>different to the whole trial population in<br>PEGASUS for the composite end point of<br>cardiovascular death, MI or stroke.|Uncertainty in the cost-effectiveness results<br>for the affected subpopulations is unknown|



## **Table 3: Other issues for information** 

|**Issue**|**Comments**|
|---|---|
|**No event state health cost**|In the**company’s**model, patients do not incur any costs while in the ‘no event’ state.  The<br>**ERG**considers that all patients will incur a health state cost, for example for outpatient<br>consultations, regardless of their health state. Previous NICE appraisal of ticagrelor (TA420)<br>applied a cost of £160.31 per cycle to individuals in the‘no event’health state. The**ERG**|





<!-- page 425 -->

||inflated this cost to a 2018 estimate (£167.66) and applied it in the ERG base case analysis<br>and the**technical team**agrees with this approach.|
|---|---|
|**Uncertainty around health- related**<br>**quality of life due to high proportion**<br>**of missing data**|**1. Utility for event free population**<br>The utility values in the company’s model were adjusted for age using utility multipliers. The<br>**ERG**notes that the baseline utility score for the event-free population of COMPASS and the<br>three subpopulations are higher than that of the UK general population for the 64-75 age group<br>(0.779). In the**ERG b**ase case, the**ERG**scales down the baseline event-free utilities, so that<br>these utilities are no higher than the UK general population.<br>**2. The multiplicative assumption (for utility values) in the economic model’s health**<br>**states**<br>The COMPASS trial was not powered for the 3 subpopulations the company is seeking<br>recommendation for. The**company**explored a multiplicative assumption in a scenario analysis<br>in which utilities of two health states are multiplied and an assumption using the utility score of<br>the most recent event is used. The**ERG**also favoured this multiplicative approach to calculate<br>utility values for the subpopulations in cases where patients suffer a second major event. The<br>**ERG**considered this more appropriate than the company’s approach of using the lowest utility<br>of the two health states following transition to another main event and considered it a better<br>representation of reality in the event of comorbidities.<br>The**technical team**agrees with the amendments made by the ERG in its base case analysis.|
|**Treatment Interruption**|In the**company’s**base case, no treatment interruption for rivaroxaban + aspirin was explicitly<br>considered after a main event (MI, stroke, CV death). However, in clinical practice, people may<br>be initiated on dual antiplatelet therapy after an MI during the acute period.<br>The company considers its approach is conservative and overestimates costs in the<br>rivaroxaban + aspirin arm. The**ERG**also notes that it is not reflective if clinical practice.<br>The**ERG**included treatment interruption in their base case which showed that the model was<br>not sensitive to this change and did not have any significant impact on the ICER. However, the<br>**technical teams’**preference is for treatment interruption to be included in the base case<br>analysis as this is reflective of clinical practice.|
|**Equality considerations**|No equalities issues were identified.|
|**Innovation**|The**company**states that there have been few advances in antithrombotic therapy for<br>secondary prevention of cardiovascular events over several decades. In the COMPASS overall<br>population, the benefit of rivaroxaban+ aspirin treatment is of a similar magnitude to other<br>accepted secondary prevention regimens such as aspirin, lipid-lowering, blood-pressure|





<!-- page 426 -->

lowering, and ACE inhibitors. The benefit is even greater in the subgroups for whom the company seeks recommendation The **ERG** notes rivaroxaban is not an innovative treatment in terms of its mechanism of action, as it is similar to other drugs used in the management of CAD for a number of years. One clinical expert consulted by the **ERG** commented that the additional benefit of rivaroxaban added to aspirin as shown in the COMPASS is regarded as an important clinical effectiveness innovation.
